Memorable Decision Cues are More Associable With Value

To fix (2)

To fix Marginal Significance

What we found

You described 1 effect with terms related to ‘marginally significant’.

Show details

You described effects with terms related to ‘marginally significant’. If p values above 0.05 are interpreted as an effect, you inflate the alpha level, and increase the Type 1 error rate. If a p value is higher than the prespecified alpha level, it should be interpreted as a non-significant result.

Text Section Header
We found a marginally significant linear relationship between memorability and correct recall across all trials (β = .06, SE = .03, p = .09). 2.2 Experiment 1B

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate at which non-significant p-values are interpreted as marginally significant, see:

Olsson-Collentine A, van Assen MALM, Hartgerink CHJ (2019). “The Prevalence of Marginally Significant Results in Psychology Over Time.” Psychological Science, 30, 576–586. doi:10.1177/0956797619830326.

For the list of terms used to identifify marginally significant results, see this blog post by Matthew Hankins.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 3 imprecise p values out of 36 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
p < .01 We found that indeed, the overall memorability (roughly shared between both images) strongly predicted the probability of choosing the higher value option (β = .14, SE = .05, p < .01), consistent with P1 (Fig. 3A).
p < 0.05 Participants were included in our final sample according to the following criteria: (1) 100% performance on instruction comprehension, AI-screening, and attention checks; (2) full experiment completion with valid responses for at least 80% of trials; and finally (3) overall performance significantly above chance (one-sided binomial test, p < 0.05).
p < 0.05 For Experiment 3, this performance criterion was replaced to match the inclusion criteria from Li et al. ( 2022):(1) the probability of choosing the rightmost option was not more than 2 standard deviations from the sample mean, (2) response times were above 300 and below 3000ms on more than half of trials, and (3) a positive (p < 0.05) effect of the difference in reported value between choice options on participants’ choices.

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (3)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 19 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of misinterpretations of non-significant results is high, see:

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

README Files

README files are a way to document the contents and structure of a folder, helping users locate the information they need. You can use a README to document changes to a repository, and explain how files are named. Please consider adding a README to each repository or including ‘README’ in the name of your overview document.

Registration’s Source Project Is Closed

1 OSF source project is closed. The files metacheck lists below were not read from it — they were retrieved from the OSF registration’s own copy, which is separate from and unaffected by the project’s access setting. Nothing is currently lost, but this is still worth fixing: a closed source project means the files only survive for as long as the registration’s copy does, and anyone who follows the project link itself (rather than the registration) will find it inaccessible.

  • https://osf.io/8d2cb is closed; its files were retrieved from the OSF registration https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/Z9Y5F, OSF.IO/Z9Y5F.

Consider making the source project openly available.

Study/Repository Mismatch

The studies named in the manuscript do not match how the repository’s files are grouped: the manuscript names studies (ex1, ex1a, ex1b, ex2, ex2a, ex2b, ex3) with no matching files in the repository; the repository separates out studies (pilot1, ex8) not named in the manuscript. Check that every study has its own clearly-named folder or file prefix, and that the folder names match how the manuscript refers to each study.

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

First, we confirmed that our participants were able to successfully learn cue-value associations in this task, replicating previous work (Duncan and Shohamy, 2016).

We found strong evidence for this (linear) interaction between memorability and value (β = .2, SE = .04, p < .001); participants are more likely to choose $1 cards and avoid $0 cards when they are more memorable.

However, on old trials, participants were now instructed to select which card they had previously seen (recognition memory; Fig. 1).

We also found a significant relationship between value and correct recall, such that participants were more likely to correctly recall a $1 association than a $0 association (β = .27, SE = .03, p < .001).

We note that this interaction is supported by the results of Experiment 1, but there participants had to infer the expected value of a novel foil.

On matched-value trials, we found a reliable effect of overall (shared) value on the probability of choosing the higher memorability card (β = 0.26, SE = 0.04, p < .001); consistent with P2, participants were more likely to choose the more memorable card when both were $1 and more likely to choose the less memorable card when both were $0 (Fig. 3B).

In their initial analysis, Li et al. (2022) reported no evidence for an overall memorability bias, consistent with our theoretical and empirical results above; participants were not more likely, overall, to choose foods cued with more memorable images.

This result held even on a subset of trials pairing two foods for which a participant gave similar preference ratings.

We recruited five independent samples of online participants via Prolific.

Eligibility was restricted to Prolific users residing in the United States who reported English as their first language and had an approval rating of at least 99% on previous Prolific studies across at least 50 previous submissions.

All participants provided informed consent, and all procedures were approved by the Columbia University Institutional Review Board.

We applied preregistered exclusion criteria to each sample (see Inclusion Criteria), with a final sample size of N = 100 participants per experiment.

On “new” (encoding) trials, both cards depicted images the participant had not yet seen, and the participant learned the value of the card they chose.

While memorability bins were fixed by these sequences, the particular images assigned to each trial were randomized for each participant.

Then, regardless of whether the recognition judgment was correct, the old card was shown alone and participants had 2 seconds to make a 2-alternativeforced-choice value memory report, between $0 and $1.

Second, during the choice phase, participants had 3 seconds to choose between two previously-rated food items (using a computer keyboard), imagining they would eat a snack-sized portion of the selected food item.

Participants were included in our final sample according to the following criteria: (1) 100% performance on instruction comprehension, AI-screening, and attention checks; (2) full experiment completion with valid responses for at least 80% of trials; and finally (3) overall performance significantly above chance (one-sided binomial test, p < 0.05).

A significant main effect of memorability bin indicates that participants are more likely to choose more memorable old cards regardless of their value (consistent with a memorability bias).

Overall memorability × value difference: participants are more sensitive to value differences between memorable cues.

  1. In Experiment 1B, participants had stronger recognition and value recall performance for more memorable cue images.

Ethics approval statement:

All participants provided informed consent, and all procedures were approved by the Columbia University Institutional Review Board.

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

z9y5f Each experiment 1-5 will sample 100 post-exclusion participants (see inclusion criteria below). Because of our within-subject design, all analyses are balanced across all 100 participants.

Experiment 5a will sample 500 post-exclusion participants in order to increase precision in this more naturalistic experiment.
Field Preregistration 1
template_name OSF Preregistration
title Cue Memorability Drives Associative Retrieval of Value
id z9y5f
link https://osf.io/z9y5f
date_created 2026-09-22T18:23:31.372595
date_modified 2026-09-17T15:05:41.071340
date_registered 2026-09-22T18:23:31.348938
embargo_end_date
ia_url https://archive.org/details/osf-registrations-z9y5f-v1
research_questions 1. Are more memorable images better cues for retrieving associated values? That is, are people’s choices more sensitive to associated values when a cue image is more memorable? (Experiments 1,2,3,4)
2. Are more memorable images more likely to be chosen, regardless of their associated value? (Experiments 1,4)
3. Does cue memorability influence value-based choices even in the domain of food choices, where preferences are subjective, choice options are highly familiar, and values are associated with categories rather than specific image cues? (Experiment 5)

These research questions and corresponding hypotheses are operationalized in the different experiments as follows (see “Statistical Models” for exact model specifications):

Experiment 1
H1.1 Participants will make more accurate choices for more memorable old cards, reflecting stronger retrieval of the card’s associated value — i.e., an interaction between memorability and value in predicting old-card choice.
H1.2: Participants will be overall more likely to choose old cards with more memorable images, independent of their associated value.

Experiment 2
H2.1: Recognition memory (correctly identifying which of the two cards was seen before) will be more accurate for more memorable images.
H2.2: Explicit value recall (correctly reporting whether an old card was worth $0 or $1) will be more accurate for more memorable images. This finding would rule out a recognition-driven account of the effects and provide unambiguous support for stronger associative memory for the value of more memorable images.

Experiment 3
H3.1: Participants will make more accurate (higher-expected-value) choices between two previously-seen cards when both cards are high-memorability than when both are low-memorability, consistent with H1.1.
H3.2: Alternatively, if memorability does not interact with value, there should be no difference between these two conditions.
(“even” trials)

Experiment 4
H4.1: When two old cards have the same value, participants will be more likely to select the high-memorability card when both are $1, and more likely to select the low-memorability card when both are $0 (i.e., interaction between overall value and memorability on choice). This would indicate that participants are more strongly influenced by their memory for the memorable image’s value, rather than an overall bias towards choosing the memorable image.
H4.2: Alternately, participants will be more likely to choose the higher memorability card, regardless of image value.

Experiment 5
H5.1: Even in food choices, where value associations have been learned over many experiences, more memorable images (i.e. the memorability of both images) result in decisions that are more consistent with reported values. This predicts an interaction between value difference and overall memorability.
H5.2: When both options have similar high values, participants are more likely to choose the more memorable image but when both options have similar low values, participants are more likely to choose the less memorable image (interaction between memorability and overall value).
H5.3: When both options have the same value, participants are more likely to choose the more memorable image (i.e. overall memorability bias, in contrast to H5.2).
foreknowledge_of_data_or_evidence Data does not yet exist. No part of the data that will be used for this analysis plan exists, and no part will be generated until after this plan is registered.
explanation_of_foreknowledge_and_managing_unintended_influences The hypotheses, exclusion criteria, and power analysis in this registration were informed by our own previously-collected pilot data across all four experiments; the new sample to be analyzed under this registration is independent and does not yet exist.
study_type Randomized Experiment: Must include random assignment of subjects to treatments or conditions. This usually includes lab experiments, field experiments, intervention experiments, randomized controlled trials, and A/B testing.
intention_for_causal_interpretation Indirect inference on causal relationship(s): This study is intended to examine a potential causal relationship, but is not designed to isolate the specific causal effect. This work may inform causal inference indirectly or advance insight toward future estimation of a causal relationship.
blinding Subjects will not be aware of the assigned treatment during data collection (either because the subjects are not human participants or because of blinding procedures). Researchers who interact with the subjects (human or non-human) will not be aware of the assigned treatments during data collection.
additional_blinding_during_research_or_analysis Subjects are not randomly assigned to conditions of interest but rather serve as their own controls for within-subjects analysis. The trial sequences themselves could be considered conditions but differences between trial sequences are not the target of analysis.
randomization This is almost entirely a within-subject design; there is no between-subjects random assignment to conditions of interest and thus every subject in each experiment sees all trial conditions relevant for the analysis. Subjects are assigned to random sequences of trials (generated via mixed integer programming), random assignments of which particular images occur (given fixed memorability bin assignments), and random left/right screen placements.
data_collection_procedures Data will be collected from a convenience sample on Prolific, using independent samples for each of the experiments. Eligibility requirements include currently residing in the United States and speaking English as a first language. AI-agent and inattentive-response mitigation include a Cloudflare Turnstile challenge before the task begins, a hidden-instruction filter for AI-based agents, attention checks used for data inclusion, and a mandatory 100% correct instructions quiz before the task begins.
sample_size_rationale This sample size is a conservative estimate determined by our pilot experiments. For completeness, we include a power analysis below for our main analysis of interest, H1.2, on the interaction of memorability with value on old trial choices in Experiment 1.

We determined sample size via a parametric-bootstrap power analysis targeting our primary hypothesis: the interaction between memorability and value in predicting old-card choice in Experiment 1 (H1.1). We fit the confirmatory generalized linear mixed-effects model to Experiment 1’s pilot data (n=106 included participants, 16,253 old-trial observations), then used the fitted model’s estimated fixed effects and random-effect variances as data-generating parameters to simulate new datasets under the same design. For each of several candidate sample sizes, we randomly subsampled that many participants’ worth of simulated data, refit the model, and recorded whether the interaction term reached significance (p < .05, two-tailed); this was repeated 150 times per candidate N. Power exceeded 80% between N=20 and N=30, and plateaued near 99–100% by N=50–60. We selected N=100 per experiment—well above the ~80%-power threshold—for conservativeness, and to match sample sizes in other studies in the literature.
stopping_rule Recruitment will continue for each experiment until the post-exclusion included sample size reaches the target size (see “Sample Size” above).
manipulated_variables Experiments 1 & 2
Memorability bin of old cards (high/middle/low) and their matched lures
Values of the cards learned during encoding ($0 or $1)
Experiment 3
Memorability bin of the old cards, always matched (low/high)
Values of the old cards (one $0, one $1 on each old trial)
Experiment 4
Memorability bin of the old cards (one high, one low)
Values of the old cards ($0 or $1, even or uneven)
Experiment 5
Memorability bin of pair of food images (both high, both low, one high/one low)
Subjective values of pair of foods (similar low, similar high, one high/one low)
measured_variables In all experiments, primary outcome measures will be choice and response time on old trials.
indices In experiment 1, accurate choices denote selecting an old card worth $1 or avoiding one worth $0. In experiment 2, accuracy is defined separately for 2-AFC recognition decisions and 2-AFC value recall decisions. In experiments 3 and 4, accurate choices denote selecting the more valuable card (note that accuracy is undefined on “even” trials in experiment 3).
transformations For modeling purposes, we will center old values so that a value of 0 is transformed to -1. Memorability bins enter models either as an ordered 2 or 3-level factor (low < mid < high) with polynomial contrasts. In the 2-level case, this is equivalent to a centered linear coding where low = -1 and high = 1.
inference_criteria Inference is based on the Wald z-test p-value for the corresponding fixed-effect coefficient (or interaction term) from the relevant GLMM, at α = .05, two-tailed. The subject-level chance-performance screen used for data exclusion (see below) uses a one-tailed binomial test against 0.5.
data_exclusion_criteria We will exclude participants who meet any of the following criteria:
Failed or responded incorrectly to any of 3 attention check trials (including responses that match hidden-text prompt designed to identify AI-agents)
Non-response rate of above 20% of trials
Failure to reliably learn associations between images and values. We will conduct a binomial test to determine if overall subject accuracy is significantly above chance (50%), which is unbiased with respect to the hypothesized analyses of memorability.
missing_data_handling Choice trials will end if subjects fail to respond within two seconds. We will exclude those trials from further analysis. If a participant does not complete the whole experiment, all their data is excluded.
study_design_overview All five experiments are within-subject repeated-measures designs implemented as self-paced, single-session online tasks (jsPsych, data collected via Prolific). In the first four experiments, every participant is assigned a full trial sequence drawn from a small set of pre-computed sequences (see below); the only participant-to-participant randomization is (a) which of these pre-computed trial sequences they receive, (b) which specific images from the memorability-binned stimulus pool are assigned to which trial slot, and (c) left/right screen placement. There are no between-subjects conditions of interest.

Stimulus Set (Experiments 1-4)
Stimuli are drawn from the THINGS image database (Hebart et al. 2019) using THINGS memorability scores (memorability_cr, the corrected-recognition memorability score; Kramer et al., 2023). “THINGS is a hierarchically structured dataset containing 26,107 images representing 1854 object concepts (such as aardvark, tank, and zucchini)…, 1619 of which are assigned to 27 higher categories (such as animal, weapon, and food)” (Kramer et al., 2023).
We use images from the 26 higher categories that have at least 6 object concepts (excluding “insects”). From those, we choose six images from each higher category, two in each of three memorability bins (low, medium and high). We use mixed integer linear programming (MILP) to choose images that maximize the difference in memorability between these bins under the constraint that no two images are of the same object concept. This results in 26 × 3 × 6 = 468 images total (156 per bin). Specifically, the low bin is selected to minimize memorability score, the high bin to maximize it, and the mid bin to minimize |score − sample median|.

In the resulting set, mean (range) memorability_cr by bin is: low = 0.550 (0.410–0.692), mid = 0.795 (0.789–0.805), high = 0.952 (0.881–1.000). Experiments 1 & 2 use all 468 images from all 3 bins, whereas experiments 3 & 4 use only the low and high bins.

Sequence pre-computation (Experiments 1-4)
The trial-level structure (old/new status, memorability bin, delay, value, and — where applicable — cross-condition pairing) is fixed in advance by the MILP solver. Ten independent MILP solutions are pre-computed per experiment (Experiment 2 uses the exact same sequences as Experiment 1); a participant is assigned one of the ten deterministically via a hash of their participant ID, so that participants are spread roughly evenly across the ten available sequences. The purpose of this design is to strictly control for possible interactions between the variables of interest and potential confounds. Details of which variables are balanced for which tasks are included below.

Additional design details for each experiment are described below:

Experiment 1
In experiment 1, we test whether image memorability drives stronger memory for associated values. To do so, we implement the card game described in Duncan & Shohamy (2016), dropping the contextual cues from the original design. The task consists of 312 trials, and includes all 468 selected stimulus images. Half of trials (156) are “new,” consisting of two novel cards, in which the chosen one flips over to reveal its value. The other half (156) are “old” trials, in which the participant chooses between a previously chosen card and a novel foil (and once again receives feedback). The delay between a new trial and its subsequent old trial is 7-15 trials. On all trials, both images presented are of the same memorability bin. Accurate choices on old trials are defined as choosing $1 cards or avoiding $0 cards (i.e., choosing the novel card).

The full trial structure is solved offline by a two-phase MILP. The distributions of delays are precisely matched across memorability bins and old card values; left/right position of the old card is also balanced within participants. To limit large strings of consecutive trials of the same memorability bin or trial type, no more than 3 consecutive same-memorability trials can occur in a row, nor more than 8 consecutive old or new trials (to accommodate the first 7 trials which must be new).

Each choice trial consists of up to 2 seconds to respond (using the ‘j’ and ‘k’ keys), followed by a 1-second highlight of the chosen card. It then flips over and reveals its value ($0 or $1) for 1.5 seconds. A “too slow” message appears if no choice is made, and a randomly selected card flips over. The inter-trial interval is 0.5 seconds. Three attention checks are interspersed in the trial sequence. Participants are paid a performance-dependent bonus; 10 old-card trials are sampled at random at the end of the session and their outcomes summed, normalized by the maximum possible ($10), and scaled to a $2 maximum bonus on top of a $5 base payment for an estimated 25-minute session.

We test for an overall bias of memorability on choice (are participants more likely to choose more memorable old cards?), and based on our hypotheses, an interaction between memorability and value (are participants more likely to make accurate choices for more memorable old cards?). See “Research Questions” section above for more details and hypotheses.

Experiment 2
In experiment 2, we isolate recognition memory from associative (value) memory, which are conflated within a single choice on experiment 1’s old trials. This experiment reuses experiment 1’s exact trial sequences and stimulus set (312 trials, all three memorability bins, the same 10 pre-computed sequences), changing only what happens on old trials. New trials are unchanged. On old trials, participants first make a forced-choice recognition judgment (“which of these two cards have you seen before?”, up to 2s), see feedback via a colored highlight (green if correct, red if incorrect, 0.75s) — and then, regardless of whether the recognition judgment was correct, report the old card’s value from memory ($0/$1, up to 2s, 0.75s feedback), with the two response keys fixed to left/right screen position rather than tied to the recognition choice or the old card’s actual side.

The bonus on this task is accuracy-based rather than reward-based: overall accuracy across all recognition and value-report trials is pooled, chance-adjusted (0.5 subtracted and rescaled to 0–1), and scaled to a $2 maximum bonus on top of a $4 base payment, for an estimated 20-minute session. We test whether recognition accuracy is higher for more memorable images, and whether value-report accuracy is higher for more memorable images — including restricted to trials where the card was correctly recognized, to rule out recognition itself as the driver. See “Research Questions” above for hypotheses.

Experiment 3
In experiment 3, we test whether the interaction between memorability and value found in experiment 1 is still present when deciding between two old cards (independent of the novel foil card in experiment 1). To do so, we modify the old trials so that both cards have previously been chosen and are now equally familiar: participants choose between two previously-seen cards, one whose source trial was worth $1 and one whose source trial was worth $0. Accurate choices are defined as choosing the $1 card. Both cards on an old trial are always from the same memorability bin, so a bias toward the more memorable card cannot by itself produce a correct answer. For analytical simplicity, we restrict this experiment to the high and low memorability bins (dropping mid); each bin’s 156 images are consumed exactly once per participant.

The task consists of 234 trials: 156 “new” encoding trials (78 high-memorability pairs, 78 low-memorability pairs, each split 39 $1 / 39 $0) and 78 “old” trials (39 high-memorability pairs, 39 low-memorability pairs). Because each old trial draws on two previously-chosen cards rather than one, every new trial’s chosen card is later reused as a source (156 new trials ÷ 2 sources per old trial = 78 old trials, with no leftover sources). As in experiment 1, delay (7–15 trials) is matched across memorability bins and value conditions, left/right position and which side carries the longer delay are balanced within participants, and the same run-length caps apply (no more than 3 consecutive same-bin trials, no more than 8 consecutive same-type trials).

Trial timing, attention checks, and the bonus mechanism are identical to experiment 1, scaled to an estimated 20-minute session with a $4 base payment and $2 maximum bonus. We test for an interaction of memorability and value — are participants more likely to make accurate choices on high-memorability trials than low-memorability trials? See “Research Questions” above for hypotheses.

Experiment 4
In experiment 4, we test how memorability influences choice when the two options’ values are similar or equal, rather than always favoring one clear answer. This offers the cleanest test of the overall “memorability bias” —are participants more likely to choose the memorable image, when values are exactly matched? Or alternatively, is choice bias modulated by value—choosing the more memorable card when both are $1, and choosing the less memorable card when both are $0 (consistent with making a choice preferentially weighted by the retrieved value of the memorable card). As in experiment 2, we restrict images to the high and low memorability bins and old trials pit two previously-chosen cards against each other, but here the two cards are always drawn from different bins — one high-memorability source and one low-memorability source. Old trials fall into four types: two “even” types where both cards share the same value (both $1 or both $0), and two “uneven” types where one card is $1 and the other $0 (i.e., there is a correct answer).

The task consists of 234 trials: 156 new encoding trials (78 high-memorability, 78 low-memorability, each split 39 $1 and 39 $0) and 78 old trials—40 “even” (20 both $0, 20 both $1) and 38 “uneven” (19 high = $1, low = $0; 19 high = $0, low = $1). Delay distributions are matched separately within the even and uneven groups so that the high- and low-memorability bins end up with identical overall delay distributions; the high-memorability source has the longer delay on exactly half of the uneven trials and half of the even trials. The same run-length caps as experiments 1–2 apply. Trial timing, the bonus mechanism, session length/pay, and the fixed attention-check positions match experiment 2.

On even trials, we test whether participants are biased toward choosing the more memorable card overall, and whether that bias is modulated by value (e.g., toward the memorable card when both are worth $1, away from it when both are worth $0). The main purpose of the uneven trials is so participants cannot predict that all old trials have no optimal choice and must engage in memory retrieval consistently, although we do not have strong hypotheses about performance. See “Research Questions” above.

Experiment 5
We replicate the sequential food-choice paradigm from Li et al. (2025) to test for our hypothesized interactions (see H5.1/H5.2 under “Research Questions” above). We will leverage the memorability scores estimated in Experiment 1A of Li et al. in our design and analysis. We will follow the design of Experiment 1B in which subjects first complete a rating phase (where they rate each food option) followed by a choice phase (where they choose between two food options). In order to more cleanly test our hypotheses about memorability, we will use only a portion of the 138 images used in Li et al. (2022). We will split the image set into terciles by measured memorability scores, and only use images in the lowest and highest terciles. This will produce a final stimulus set of 92 images: 46 high-memorability and 46 low-memorability.

In choosing the pairing of options, Li et al. (2022) designed two trial types. In similar value trials, we will follow exactly the pairing procedure of Li et al. (2022): all 92 images will be ranked by a participant’s reported preferences, and adjacent ranks will be paired to be displayed together on choice trials. This procedure ignores memorability, but will likely produce similar-value trials both across- and within-memorability bin. These similar-value trials (akin to “even trials” in Experiment 4) will test for an interaction between the overall value and difference in memorability.

In distant value trials, Li et al. (2022) paired each ranked image with one at a fixed interval away (e.g., image 1 with image 70, image 2 with image 71, etc.). We will follow the same procedure, but only within each memorability bin (e.g., high-mem image 1 with high-mem image 23, high-mem image 2 with high-mem image 24; low-mem image 1 with low-mem image 23, low-mem image 2 with low-mem image 24). These distant value trials will test for an interaction between overall memorability and difference in value.

Experiment 5a
The design of experiment 5a is the same as experiment 5 with one small change to the distant-value condition. In the distant-value condition, we pair each ranked image (within memorability bin) with another one at a fixed interval away. In 5a, we use an interval of 8 rather than 23 (as above), in order to make choices slightly less consistent, while still having an option that is systematically preferred. We believe this will allow sufficient variability in these choices in order to properly test for an interaction with memorability.
sample_size Each experiment 1-5 will sample 100 post-exclusion participants (see inclusion criteria below). Because of our within-subject design, all analyses are balanced across all 100 participants.

Experiment 5a will sample 500 post-exclusion participants in order to increase precision in this more naturalistic experiment.
statistical_tests We will fit these models using the glmer function in lme4. For models that do not converge, we will follow the algorithm from Bates et al. (2015) to find the maximal model that converges. We will then follow Bates in using likelihood ratio tests to select an appropriate model.

Experiment 1:
old_value: centered value of old option (1 if value = $1, -1 if value = $0)
memorabilty_bin: low, medium, high (orthogonal polynomial contrasts)
old_image: ID of old image
new_image: ID of new image

glmer(chose_old ~ old_value * memorability_bin +
(old_value * memorability_bin | participant_id) + (old_value | old_image) + (1 | new_image), family = binomial)

Experiment 2:
old_value: centered value of old option (1 if value = $1, -1 if value = $0)
memorabilty_bin: low, medium, high (orthogonal polynomial contrasts)
old_image: ID of old image
new_image: ID of new image

glmer(correct_recognition ~ old_value * memorability_bin +
(old_value * memorability_bin | participant_id) + (old_value | old_image) + (1 | new_image), family = binomial)
glmer(correct_recall ~ old_value * memorability_bin +
(old_value * memorability_bin | participant_id) + (old_value | old_image) + (1 | new_image), family = binomial)

Experiment 3:
memorability_bin: 1 if high, -1 if low
high_image: ID of image associated with high value
low_image: ID of image associated with low value
glmer(correct ~ memorability_bin +
(memorability_bin | participant_id) + (1 | high_image) + (1 | low_image),
family = binomial)

Experiment 4:
high_mem_value: 1 if high memorability image associated with $1, -1 if high memorability image associated with $0
high_mem_image: ID of high memorability image
low_mem_image: ID of low memorability image
glmer(chose_high_mem ~ overall_value +
(overall_value | participant_id) + (overall_value | high_mem_image) + (overall_value | low_mem_image),
family = binomial)

Experiment 5:
Values will be z-scored within participant.
chose_right: chose option on right
delta_value: right option value - left option value
overall_value: right option value + left option value
delta_memorability: right image memorability - left image memorability
overall_memorability: right image memorability + left image memorability

We will first fit models separately by trial type (similar-value and distant-value). On similar value trials, we will fit:

glmer(chose_right ~ delta_memorability * overall_value +
(delta_memorability * overall_value | participant_id) + (1 | right_image) + (1 | left_image), family = binomial)

And on distant value trials, we will fit:
glmer(chose_right ~ delta_value * overall_memorability +
(delta_value * overall_memorability | participant_id) + (1 | right_image) + (1 | left_image), family = binomial)

Finally, we will fit the following model on the entire dataset:

glmer(chose_right ~ delta_value * overall_memorability + delta_memorability * overall_value +
(delta_value * overall_memorability + delta_memorability * overall_value | participant_id) + (1 | right_image) + (1 | left_image), family = binomial)

Experiment 5a:
Analysis will use the same approach as Experiment 5.
paper_id input

How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.), reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000), or impossible (below 0 or above 1, e.g., p = -.22).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold”, “legend” or “named”).

A p-value with the name of a correction or a test after the p (“pFWE < .05”, “padj < 0.05”, “p Wilcoxon = .02”) is flagged only when a test statistic comes before it in its clause (“t(37) = 7.17, p FWE < .05”). Without one it is mostly the rule that decided what was significant (“genes with padj < 0.05 were selected”); such a p-value is “named” in not_a_test. Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

A p-value below 0 or above 1 is impossible; it is listed apart and not also as imprecise. Often the statistic before it, or its minus sign, was copied into it (“t(134) = -2.32, p = -.13”, “Z = 3.091, P = 3.091”). A value above 1 is listed only after a test statistic in its clause: without one, a “P” above 1 was nearly always another quantity (a proportion, pH, a group’s mean, a model parameter). A number in scientific notation whose power of ten was lost when the PDF was read (“p = 2.4 x 10 -9” read as 2.4) is not listed. A “p” that is the subscript of another symbol (“r p = .64”, “²p = .02” for partial eta squared, “Δp = .33”), or a quantity with a unit, a logarithm or a ratio (“P = 2.9 µm”, “-log10 P = 7.5”, “Ca/P = 1.67”), is not read as a p-value at all (see extract_p_values()). Each p-value has its own row, also when the same one is written twice in a sentence.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: Validated on 226 Psychological Science papers with 5,019 hand-coded p-values, 438 of them imprecise (scienceverse/validation, branch stat_p_exact). Version 0.4.4.3 reported 419 p-values as imprecise, 388 of them correctly (93%), and missed 50 (89% found). Of the misses, 13 are starred table notes (“*p < .05”) that the module leaves out on purpose and 11 are p-values it did not find; of the wrong reports, 20 are p-values the ground truth does not contain (such as “ps < .05”) and 6 use ≤ or ≥. These figures measure agreement with the hand-coding, which has known errors in both directions (scienceverse/validation#48).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

A p-value in a table note (“† p < .10, * p < .05”) or a figure legend (“dashed lines indicate non-significant paths (p > .05)”) is left out, as in stat_p_exact; the column not_a_test says why. A p-value next to words that state a threshold is kept, because such sentences often report a result against that level (“not significant at the conventional threshold (p = .054)”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: Validated on three hand-coded sets of papers (scienceverse/validation: 249 Psychological Science, 250 Economics and 250 MDPI papers). Of 1,783 non-significant p-values in the ground truth, version 0.4.4.3 found 1,719 (3.6% missed), and 94.8% of its 1,814 detections were in the ground truth. Some differences come from the ground truth: it lacks p-values with a correction name (“p Bonf = .061”), and counts partial eta squared written with its subscript after it (“η 2 p = .05”), “p = .05”, and some table notes and figure legends (“† p < .10, *p < .05”), which this module leaves out, as non-significant p-values. These figures measure agreement with the hand-coding, which has known errors in both directions (scienceverse/validation#48).

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

Whose deposit is each link? The ownership table gives, for every repository linked in the text and (with references = TRUE) every deposit cited in the reference list, one of six labels: own_this (made by the authors for this paper), own_earlier_data or own_earlier_code (the authors’ own, from earlier work), others_data or others_code (made by other people, reused here), or not_data (a publication such as a preprint, not a deposit), with ownership_group (“own”, “others”, “not_data”) and the reason. It is decided without a language model, from the sentence or reference that cites the link, the paper’s authors (names, ORCID iDs, affiliations), and what the deposit’s own record and files say (who made it, what it names, when, whether it is a software package; for an OSF project cited only in the reference list, its record alone). On a round of labelled links the rules had not been written from, the three groups were right for 98% of links in the main text (93 of 95) and 90% in reference lists (74 of 82); the labels were made by Claude agents from each link’s evidence, and disputed ones checked against the deposits. Telling this paper’s own deposit from the authors’ earlier one is less certain.

Only the deposits that are this paper’s own are checked. A deposit linked in the text that holds other people’s work (others_data, others_code) or the authors’ earlier work (own_earlier_data, own_earlier_code) is listed in the report, with the reason, but its files are left out of table and kept in table_not_checked: they count toward nothing here (files that do not belong in a repository, naming, READMEs, studies, the traffic light), and code_check, data_check and reproducibility_check, which read table, do not check them either. reproducibility_check still puts the files of theirs that the paper’s code names in the folder the code runs in, as a re-analysis needs the data it re-analyses. When every linked deposit is set aside, the traffic light is “na”. A deposit that is the paper’s own is counted as such when its README, title or description names the paper (its title or DOI).

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Levi Baruch

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI.
