If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

When making decisions, we must use the sensory cues available at the moment of choice to predict the values of our options. Yet little is known about how properties of these cues affect the choice process, particularly our ability to retrieve decision-relevant memories. Here, we test whether and how image memorability, the population likelihood of recognizing a particular image, influences memory-guided decisions. Across five pre-registered experiments, memorable cue images were not chosen more overall; instead, they predicted more accurate retrieval of associated values and more value-sensitive choices. We account for this pattern of behavior with a formal exemplar model in which distinctive cues suffer less interference from competing memories during retrieval of associated values. The model predicts, and our data confirm, two interactions: choices are more sensitive to value differences between memorable options, and a difference in memorability biases choices toward the more memorable option when both are high in value, but away from it when both are low. Finally, we find that the same pattern held for choices between common foods, suggesting that shared associative memory processes are recruited for choices involving both novel and highly familiar options. Together, our results suggest that more memorable cues are more associable with value, and provide a process-level account for how memorability influences downstream decisions.

1 Introduction

When confronted with a decision, we have rich memory traces for past experiences that we can use to predict the outcome of choice options. However, at the time of choice, we only have access to current sensory cues, such as a candy wrapper or a book cover. How do we use these cues to make a decision?

One possibility is to base decisions on memory strength, or familiarity, of the cue itself. For instance, a series of studies on the memory bias demonstrated that people tend to prefer choice options for which they have stronger memories (Gluth et al., 2015; Weilbächer et al., 2020; Kraemer et al., 2022; Mechera-Ostrovsky and Gluth, 2018). Similarly, people are more willing to bet on lotteries with more familiar cues, when they need to recall reward probabilities from memory (Vaidya and Badre, 2020; Vaidya et al., 2025). Relatedly, classic studies of the "mere exposure" effect showed that greater familiarity (either from life experience or through experimental repetition) can increase the associated value of a stimulus (Zajonc, 1968). And finally, to the extent that people spend more time looking at more memorable stimuli (Dubey et al., 2015; Sulewski et al., 2026; Chakravarthula et al., 2025) and looking time predicts preferential choice (Shimojo et al., 2003; Armel et al., 2008; Krajbich et al., 2010), we would expect a choice bias towards memorable cues, at least in choices between appetitive options (Armel et al., 2008).

Another possibility is to recruit associative memory processes to recall value information associated with a decision cue. This putative role for associative or relational memory (Weber et al., 1995; Madan et al., 2014; Shohamy and Daw, 2015; Murty et al., 2016; Wimmer and Büchel, 2016; Duncan and Shohamy, 2016; Bornstein et al., 2017; Bakkour et al., 2019) suggests that our choices are in part determined by how effectively the perceptual presentation of a choice option can be used to access information from memory. In a related line of work, Gomilsek et al. (2025) demonstrated that repeated presentation of cue-value pairs, rather than instill an overall familiarity bias, makes subsequent choices more sensitive to associated values, consistent with stronger associative retrieval.

While much research has investigated value-based choice, far less is known about how properties of a cue affect our ability to retrieve and use value information associated with it. Given the critical role of memory here, one factor of particular interest is a cue's memorability-the population-level likelihood that a given image will be recognized as having been seen before (Isola et al., 2011; Bainbridge, 2019; Bainbridge et al., 2013) foot_0 . Image memorability is remarkably consistent across individuals, and is independent of reported aesthetic preference (Isola et al., 2011; Bainbridge et al., 2013). Emerging research suggests that the effects of image memorability extend beyond recognition memory, to tasks such as paired associate learning, spatial recall, and serial order recall (Borkin et al., 2016; Wang et al., 2025; Trinkl and Wolfe, 2025; Cheng et al., 2026; Cook and Westerman, 2026). These results suggest that image memorability is related to image associability, which may be surprising in light of long-standing theoretical and functional distinctions between item and relational memory (Buchler et al., 2008; Clark and Gronlund, 1996; Raaijmakers and Shiffrin, 1981; Yonelinas, 2002). If memorable cues are indeed more associable, one underlying reason may be related to their distinctiveness: distinctive stimuli tend to be both better recognized and serve as stronger cues for associative recall (Going and Read, 1974; Busey and Tunnicliff, 1999; Konkle et al., 2010; Lockhead, 1970; Bruyer and Courvoisier, 1990; Pantelis et al., 2008).

Here, we ask: how does the memorability of decision cues influence value-based choices? One possibility is that memorable cues will be chosen more overall, a memorability bias. Alternatively, more memorable images may be more associable with value, supporting stronger retrieval of values from memory and more value-sensitive choices. 2 Across five pre-registered behavioral experiments, we demonstrate strong support for the latter, that more memorable decision cues predict better retrieval of associated value to guide decisions. In Experiment 1A, we find that memory-guided choices are more sensitive to associated values for more memorable cues. In Experiment 1B, we separate recognition memory from value recall and find that memorability affects both. These results suggest that more memorable cues are more associable with value, and that the properties of a cue affect how associated information is retrieved. We show that our data are naturally explained by an exemplar model of memory, in which cue distinctiveness mitigates interference and thus produces both greater recognition performance and more accurate associative recall. From this formal account, we derive two novel predictions about how memorability and value interact to shape choices. In Experiments 2A and 2B, we confirm these predictions empirically; choices are more sensitive to differences in value when cues are more memorable, and the effect of cue memorability differs as a function of the value of choice options, rather than leading to a simple bias towards the more memorable cue.

Finally, we test whether the same principles apply to food choices (Armel et al., 2008; Hare et al., 2009; Steinglass et al., 2015). Unlike the previous experiments, which explicitly rely on single episodic memories, food choices are putatively based on preferences learned over a lifetime of experiences, cued with highly familiar stimuli. Surprisingly, even here, we find that memorability similarly modulates the retrieval of value associations to guide choices, consistent with the predictions of our exemplar-model account. Together, our findings suggest that image memorability has cascading consequences for associative memory of value and decision-making, both for novel and highly familiar choice options, and provide a formal, process-level account of how memorability influences value-based decisions.

2 Results

All results presented follow from preregistered analyses, unless noted as exploratory (https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/Z9Y5F). In Experiment 1A, participants chose between one old card and one new card. In Experiment 1B, to disentangle recognition memory and value recall, participants first selected which card they saw before, and then which value that card was worth. In Experiment 2A, participants saw two old cards, of the same memorability bin but different value. In Experiment 2B, on "matched-value" trials, participants saw two old cards of the same value but different memorability bins, to test how memorability would bias decisions between equal-value options.

2.1 Experiment 1A

To investigate the effect of cue memorability on associative value learning and decision-making, we adapted a task from Duncan and Shohamy (2016, see Fig. 1). On each trial, participants chose between two cards, each depicting a unique object image. The chosen card then flipped over to reveal its value-either $0 or $1. Several trials later, on an "old trial," a previously-chosen card could reappear, and participants could use their memory for the card's value to guide their decision. Old trials always required memory for values learned on only a single previous trial. Card images were drawn from the THINGS dataset (Hebart et al., 2019), using pre-collected image memorability ratings (corrected recognition score: hit -false alarm, Kramer et al., 2023) to create three category-balanced memorability bins (high, medium, low; see Methods: Stimuli ).

First, we confirmed that our participants were able to successfully learn cue-value associations in this task, replicating previous work (Duncan and Shohamy, 2016). Choices on old trials reflected the rewards experienced on previous trials, as indicated by a significant overall effect of old card value on choices (β = 0.8, SE = 0.05, p < .001). We then turn to our primary question of interest: how does the memorability of a card's image influence these memory-guided choices? One possibility is an overall memorability bias: that more memorable cards are inferred to be higher value, and thus chosen more overall. Alternatively, if more memorable cards are more associable with their learned values, choices will be more sensitive to the values of higher memorability cards, predicting an interaction between value and memorability on choices. We found strong evidence for this (linear) interaction between memorability and value (β = .2, SE = .04, p < .001); participants are more likely to choose $1 cards and avoid $0 cards when they are more memorable. We also included memorability as a nonlinear predictorfoot_2 and did not find reliable evidence for nonlinear interactions between memorability and value (p = .19). Notably, we also did not find any evidence for an overall memorability bias, as indicated by a main effect of memorability (p = .73 and .83 for linear and nonlinear effects, respectively).

These data reveal an interaction between memorability and value, where cue memorability supports stronger memory for associated values retrieved at the time of choice. In this design, successful choices on old trials required both recognizing a card as old, and retrieving its associated value; we next modified the experiment to disentangle the effects of memorability on these two processes.

2.2 Experiment 1B

We modified our task to separate image recognition and associative value retrieval. The sequence of trials (including images, memorability bins, values, and delays) remained identical to Experiment 1A. However, on old trials, participants were now instructed to select which card they had previously seen (recognition memory; Fig. 1). Following immediate feedback on their choice, they were then prompted to directly report the old card's value ($0 or $1).

First, we sought to confirm that image memorability, as measured in the THINGSmem dataset (Kramer et al., 2023), predicted successful recognition memory in our task, despite relatively short encoding-retrieval delays and the 2-alternative-forced-choice format. As expected, we found a significant linear main effect of memorability on the probability of correct recognition judgment (β = .38, SE = .06, p < .001). We did not find any reliable effects of value (p = .98), nonlinear effects of memorability (p = .43) or interactions between value and memorability (ps = .18, .87) on recognition memory.

We then tested if memorability predicted successful value recall. We found a marginally significant linear relationship between memorability and correct recall across all trials (β = .06, SE = .03, p = .09). However, it stands to reason that recall of value should be successful on trials where participants correctly recognize the image as having been seen before, and indeed in Experiment 1A, successful recognition likely precedes any influence of recalled value on choices. While we did not specify this subset in our preregistration, we present this here as an exploratory analysis. On correctly-recognized old trials, the linear relationship between memorability and recall probability is now deemed reliable when using traditional thresholds (β = .08, SE = .04, p = .04). A reasonable interpretation of these results is that cue memorability predicts stronger value recall.

We also found a significant relationship between value and correct recall, such that participants were more likely to correctly recall a $1 association than a $0 association (β = .27, SE = .03, p < .001). We did not find any reliable relationships between recall and nonlinear memorability (p = .9) or the interaction between value and memorability (ps = .54, .12).

2.3 Experiment 1 Discussion

The results of Experiment 1 demonstrate that the memorability of a cue image predicts more accurate retrieval of associative values and choices that are more sensitive to these retrieved values. These results are somewhat surprising, in light of decades of work showing theoretical and functional distinctions between item and associative memory (Buchler et al., 2008; Clark and Gronlund, 1996; Raaijmakers and Shiffrin, 1981; Yonelinas, 2002). Why, then, would item memorability be related to both associative memory and choice?

The distinctiveness of decision cues offers a possible answer (Murdock Jr., 1960; Schmidt, 1991). Image distinctiveness (in relation to other stored memories) is known to facilitate recognition memory (Going and Read, 1974; Busey and Tunnicliff, 1999; Konkle et al., 2010), as well as associative recall (Lockhead, 1970; Bruyer and Courvoisier, 1990; Pantelis et al., 2008). Indeed, several studies have related image memorability to various measures of distinctiveness (Lukavský and Děchtěrenko, 2017; Bylinskii et al., 2015; Bainbridge et al., 2013; Valentine et al., 2016), and most recently Simpson et al. (2026) showed that image memorability is highly correlated with several measures of distinctiveness in the (THINGSmem Kramer et al., 2023) dataset, from which our stimuli are drawn. How, mechanistically, could cue distinctiveness give rise to the pattern of results in Experiment 1? Exemplar (or instance) models provide a process-level framework to understand these phenomena (Hintzman, 1984; Nosofsky, 1988). In these models, each experience is stored as a separate memory trace, and during retrieval, each trace is activated according to its similarity to the cue. Recognition judgments can then be computed on the summed activation ("global match") (Hintzman, 1984; Nosofsky, 1988), and reconstructed stimuli, category labels, or other associated information (such as reward) can be predicted with averages in which each memory trace is weighted by its activation (DeLosh et al., 1997; Medin and Schaffer, 1978; Nosofsky, 1986). Distinctive cues produce less interference from competing memories, and therefore more accurate memory retrieval. Indeed, recent work has used exemplar models of image distinctiveness to explain image memorability, explaining as much variance as image-based deep learning approaches (Meagher and Nosofsky, 2023; Nosofsky et al., 2025). Qualitatively, Nairne (2002) also pointed out that these models predict that distinctive images will be better for retrieving a target paired associate. However, we are currently unaware of work relating cue distinctiveness and choice. 4 Here, building on simple exemplar models of choice (Juslin and Persson, 2002; Gomilsek et al., 2025), we introduce a process-level theory for how image memorability (distinctiveness) is related to associative value recall and downstream choice. We show that this model accounts of the results of Experiment 1, and we ask what novel predictions this model offers for downstream decision-making.

2.4 An Exemplar Model Account of Value Associability

Our mathematical model (Appendix: Modeling) describes how cue memorability predicts stronger associative recall and more value-sensitive choices, offering two key contributions. First, we synthesize the ideas above by formalizing how cue distinctiveness drives stronger associative recall (following Nairne, 2002). Intuitively, cues that are less distinctive (less memorable) produce more interference when trying to retrieve associated values (Morales-Calva et al., 2025), which pulls retrieved value estimates towards the mean of interfering memories. Image distinctiveness can therefore account for the results of Experiment 1, where more memorable cues were more associable with value.

Second, we ask what novel predictions this distinctiveness-based account makes for downstream decisions. By assuming that values used in choices are retrieved in a way that depends on trace activation (following standard exemplar theories of choice, Juslin and Persson, 2002; Gomilsek et al., 2025), we derive two novel and testable predictions about how memorability and value interact to shape choices. These predictions apply not just to choices guided by single episodic memory traces (as above), but more generally to any preferential choice between familiar options. In particular, our model predicts: 1.

P1: Overall memorability × value difference: choices are more sensitive to differences in value between more memorable options, as retrieved values will be less distorted by interference.

2. P2: Memorability difference × overall value: The effect of cue memorability depends on the value of choice options. When values are similar, this presents as a reflection effect (Weilbächer et al., 2020; Kahneman and Tversky, 1979): participants prefer the more memorable cue when both are high-value and the less memorable cue when both are low-value. This arises because less memorable cues evoke more interference, and thus their retrieved values are pulled towards the mean value of competing memories. Less memorable options will therefore seem worse (when high-value), or better (when low-value) than they really are (see Appendix for more details).

Our theory predicts these interactions insofar as choices recruit episodic memory to estimate the value of choice options. Notably, under this account, memorability does not make a cue more attractive in itself, but rather sharpens retrieval, making the cue's recalled value less biased by competing memory traces. For the same reason, it predicts no main effect of overall (summed) memorability. 5Altogether, we find that exemplar models provide a natural and mechanistic account of the data from Experiment 1, describing how cue memorability relates to associative recall and downstream decisions. We now proceed to directly test the two predictions above. The first (P1) is tested in Experiment 2A, by varying the shared memorability of two choice options with a large difference in value. The second prediction (P2) is tested in Experiment 2B, by evaluating choices between equal-valued items with a large difference in memorability. Unlike Experiment 1, directly testing these predictions requires choices between two options with associated values. Thus, in both Experiments 2A and 2B, "old trial" choices included two old options, and to simplify the task design, we only used high-and low-memorability images.

2.5 Experiment 2A

In Experiment 2A, we set out to directly test for an interaction between the overall memorability of choice options and their difference in value (P1). That is, are choices more sensitive to the difference in value between two options when their overall (shared) memorability is high? We note that this interaction is supported by the results of Experiment 1, but there participants had to infer the expected value of a novel foil. In this experiment, old trials involved two previously-chosen cards, both either high-or low-memorability, with counterbalanced value, memorability, and screen location.

We found that indeed, the overall memorability (roughly shared between both images) strongly predicted the probability of choosing the higher value option (β = .14, SE = .05, p < .01), consistent with P1 (Fig. 3A). Thus, choices are more sensitive to differences in value for higher-memorability options. We note that memory-guided choices in this experiment were far from ceiling, and we predict this effect will be weakened when differences in value are so strong as to induce ceiling performance.

2.6 Experiment 2B

In Experiment 2B, we turn to the second predicted interaction (P2), between two images' overall value and their difference in memorability. That is, when two choice options have similar or even equal values, how does memorability influence which option is preferred?

To address this question, we modified our task design such that each old trial presented one highmemorability card and one low-memorability card. On half of the trials ("matched-value" trials), these two cards shared the same value (either both $0 or both $1, in equal proportions). These trials allowed us to evaluate how memorability influenced choices between equally-valued choice options. Because choices cannot be based on a difference in true associated value, they also offer a clean test of an overall memorability bias. On the remaining ("mixed-value") trials, one card was worth $0 and one worth $1 (counterbalanced across

Figure 3: Memorability and value interact to predict binary preferential decisions. Experiment 2 tested for two predicted interactions between memorability and value, in choices between two previously-chosen (old) cards. (A) In Experiment 2A, choices were more sensitive to the cards' associated values when the cue images were more memorable, supporting an interaction between the difference in value and the overall memorability (P1). (B) In Experiment 2B, on matched-value trials, participants preferred the more memorable card when both were high-value, and the less memorable card when both were low-value, reflecting more sensitivity to the value of the more memorable card. This result supports an interaction between the difference in memorability and overall value (P2). All error bars depict 95% confidence interval of the mean.

the two memorability bins). We included these trials to ensure participants attempted to retrieve value information from memory, but they are irrelevant to our main analysis of interest.

On matched-value trials, we found a reliable effect of overall (shared) value on the probability of choosing the higher memorability card (β = 0.26, SE = 0.04, p < .001); consistent with P2, participants were more likely to choose the more memorable card when both were $1 and more likely to choose the less memorable card when both were $0 (Fig. 3B). Alternatively put, the overall value interacted with the difference in memorability to predict choices. These results are inconsistent with an overall memorability bias, as choices in low-value trials were biased away from the more memorable option. Instead, they are consistent with participants having more precise associative memory for the more memorable card, and choices being more strongly driven by the value of that item.

Together, the results of Experiments 2 confirm two hypothesized interactions between memorability and value on binary choices: between the overall memorability and the difference in value (P1), and between the overall value and the difference in memorability (P2). The data are consistent with more memorable cue images producing less interference and therefore more precise associative memory for value, rather than an overall memorability bias.

2.7 Experiment 3

The experiments above evaluate how memorability affects cue-reward associations learned from a single episode within an experimental session. We then sought to test how these findings generalize to more naturalistic decisions, where choices frequently involve highly familiar options, with preferences learned over a lifetime of reward experiences, and the particular perceptual cues may vary from one encounter to another. Food choice offers a natural and well-established paradigm for investigating these questions: most people have extensive experience with common foods and make choices between them daily. It is not obvious that episodic memory (and therefore, memorability, a measure derived from episodic recognition experiments) would influence these choices at all. However, recent fMRI and patient studies suggest that episodic memory mechanisms underlie deliberation in the context of decisions about food (Bakkour et al., 2019). If valuerelated evidence from memory is retrieved to guide food choices, then the memorability of cue images during a decision would be expected to modulate choices.

We conducted an initial test of these predictions by re-analyzing a previously-collected dataset (Li et al., 2022), in which 44 participants performed recognition memory and binary food choice tasks with a standardized set of food images (Lloyd et al., 2020; Armel et al., 2008). In their initial analysis, Li et al. (2022) reported no evidence for an overall memorability bias, consistent with our theoretical and empirical results above; participants were not more likely, overall, to choose foods cued with more memorable images. This result held even on a subset of trials pairing two foods for which a participant gave similar preference ratings.

Building on this result and our findings from the reward learning experiments, we turned to investigate whether their data support the two interactions predicted by the distinctiveness-based theory presented above (see Appendix: Modeling). We first analyzed data from the similar-rating trials (analogous to our matched-value trials in Experiment 2B). We fit a model predicting whether the food option on the right side was chosen, as a function of the signed difference in memorability (right minus left) and their overall value (the sum of their normalized preference ratings). As our theory predicts (P2), we found a significant interaction between these two variables (β = .54, SE = .24, p = .02) (Fig. S3C). Consistent with Li et al. (2022)'s original analysis, we did not find a main effect of memorability (p = .5). We also did not find a reliable main effect of overall value (p = .95).

We next turned to the interaction between overall memorability and value difference: did participants make more value-sensitive choices when the overall memorability is higher (P1)? We analyzed distantrating trials, where foods were paired with relatively large differences in preference ratings. We fit a model that included both hypothesized interactions (including both was necessary, as their design did not intentionally include similar-memorability trials as in our Exp. 2A, so differences in memorability could also influence choices). While choices were sensitive to the difference in value between options (β = 1.25, SE = .09, p < .001), we did not find reliable evidence for the interaction between the difference in value and overall memorability (p = .3). Interestingly, even on these distant-value trials, we once again find a reliable interaction between overall value and difference in memorability (P2), as above (β = .66, SE = .31, p = .03). We did not find any further reliable main effects of the difference in memora- (C) On similar-value trials, participants' choices reflected more sensitivity to the value of the higher-memorability item; they were more likely to choose the more memorable cues when both options were high-value, and more likely to choose the less memorable cue when both were low-value (mirroring Exp. 2B). All error bars depict 95% confidence interval of the mean.

bility (p = .5), overall ratings (p = .31) or overall memorability (p = .93). Why might this interaction not have been present in Li et al. (2022)'s data? We hypothesized that, because the stimulus pairings ignored memorability in their task, trials did not vary enough in their overall memorability to support this analysis. Moreover, analyzing only a subset of trials in their 44-person sample may not be sufficiently powered to detect such an interaction, especially in trials where we expect most of the choice variability to be driven by strong value preferences (independent of the cues). Therefore, we conducted a preregistered follow-up study, with the same design and stimuli, and two changes: (1) we divided up the food images into three equally-sized memorability bins, and only used food options from the highest and lowest bin; and (2) in constructing the distant-rating trials, we ensured that both choice options were in the same bin, ensuring greater variability in overall memorability with relatively small differences in memorability between the options.

We first sought to replicate the interaction between overall value and the difference in memorability (P2). Across all trials in the experiment, we found a reliable interaction (β = .65, SE = .14, p < .001); the same was true for a model fit to just the subset of similar-rating trials, where we expected this effect to be clearest (β = .82, SE = .17, p < .001; Fig. 4C). Surprisingly, we also found a main effect of overall value (β = -.09, SE = .04, p = .02), suggesting that people were less likely to choose the stimulus on the right when overall value was higher. 6 . Finally, as in Li et al. (2022), we did not observe any reliable main effects of the difference in memorability in the full dataset (p = .85) or just the similar-rating trials (p = .84).

We then turned to the second interaction, between the overall memorability and the difference in value (P1). Across all trials, we found a reliable interaction (β = .07, SE = .03, p = .04; Fig. 4B). This effect was considerably weaker than the former, and when limiting this analysis to just the distant-rating trials, this interaction was no longer significant (p = .23). On these trials, we did find a large effect of the difference in value rating (β = 1.74, SE = .08, p < .001), consistent with value-sensitive choices. We also did not find any main effect of overall memorability in the full dataset (p = .48) or just on distant-value trials (p = .9).

Thus, we find evidence for both predicted interactions between memorability and value on food choices, with stronger support for P2 than P1.

3 Discussion

Decisions about value require inferring the value of choice options from past experience, using only the sensory cues available to us at the time of choice. In the present work, we investigated how the memorability of decision cues affects retrieval of associated values from memory to guide decisions. We tested two alternative accounts: first, that memorable images would be preferred more overall (memorability bias), or second, that memorable images would be more associable with value, and thus predict stronger associative retrieval of value and more value-sensitive choices.

Across our pre-registered behavioral experiments, we find strong support for the latter. In Experiment 1A, we found that decisions based on episodic memories for value were more accurate when cued with more memorable images. In Experiment 1B, we confirmed that this effect exists separately from image recognition, with stronger direct value recall for more memorable images.

These results extend and support recent demonstrations that image memorability has cascading effects on downstream associative and relational memory. A recent study of working memory found that image memorability predicted stronger performance for associations, spatial locations, and temporal order (Cheng et al., 2026); another study similarly found stronger spatial source memory judgments but no benefit for temporal source memory (Trinkl and Wolfe, 2025). Another recent study found evidence that memorable images were better associated with paired words, even after multi-day delays, and especially for semanticallyunrelated pairs (Wang et al., 2025), while another found evidence that more memorable faces were better cues for associated names but more memorable scenes were not (Cook and Westerman, 2026). These findings raise many questions about underlying mechanisms and relationships between item and associative memory. If image memorability can be read out from firing patterns in high-level visual cortex (Jaegle et al., 2019; Rust and Mehrpour, 2020; Bainbridge et al., 2017; Lahner et al., 2024), how do these representational properties affect associative memory in the hippocampus and other downstream areas?

To begin answering these questions, we propose a mechanistic cognitive model of how cue memorability supports more precise associative retrieval of value. We draw on classic exemplar models of memory (Hintzman, 1984; Nosofsky, 1988), including recent attempts to capture image-level variation in memorability (Meagher and Nosofsky, 2023; Nosofsky et al., 2025), as well as more recent applications to judgment and decision making (Juslin and Persson, 2002; Gomilsek et al., 2025). Our theory rests on the assumptions that (1) a similarity-based process over cues determines how associated values are retrieved, and (2) memorable cues are more distinctive, and thus more resistant to interference from competing memory traces. Memorable cues naturally yield more accurate estimates of associated values, and the associated values of less memorable cues are pushed towards the mean expected value. We note that the relationship between memorability and distinctiveness is still a topic of active research; some studies suggest the two are empirically distinct (Kraemer and Gluth, 2023; Bylinskii et al., 2015; Roberts and Bainbridge, 2026), whereas others suggest that the two are theoretically identical and that empirical mismatches come from an inability to properly characterize distinctiveness in the representational space in which memory retrieval operates (Simpson et al., 2026). We also note associability, by triggering retrieval of related information, could itself make items more distinctive (or memorable) in memory. Future work is necessary to clarify these relationships and the extent to which distinctiveness accounts can fully explain image memorability.

When these value estimates are used to guide choices, this model predicts two key interactions between memorability and value on choice: first, between the overall (summed) memorability and the difference in value between options, and second, between the overall (summed) value and the difference in memorability. We test these two predictions in Experiment 2. In Experiment 2A, we find the former: choices are more sensitive to the difference in value between more memorable options. In Experiment 2B, we find the latter: choices are biased towards the more memorable item when both are high-value and away from the more memorable item when both are low-value, consistent with more precise retrieval of the more memorable item's value. Together, these results confirm the presence of these two distinct interactions between memorability and value during decisions. We also note that these effects mirror those in Gomilsek et al. (2025), where cue-value pairs are repeated to strengthen associative memory, although in our experiments these effects arise from cue manipulations alone.

Our distinctiveness-based theory provides a parsimonious account of the observed relationships between item memorability on item recognition, cued associative recall, and value-based decisions. We emphasize that this account does not depend on distinctiveness explaining all of the variability in item memorability, but rather that whatever component of memorability is explained by distinctiveness may drive the relationship between recognition, recall, and choice. We also highlight that we refer to "distinctiveness" as distinctiveness in memory, which may not be well captured by particular perceptual or conceptual measures. However, we note that alternative (and potentially related) accounts may also predict the observed effects of memorability on value-based decisions. For instance, a popular alternative account of what makes stimuli memorable is that they are processed more efficiently ( (Broers et al., 2018; Deng et al., 2024; Bainbridge et al., 2026) but see Sheffi and Makovski, 2026). Although to our knowledge no mechanistic account of this idea yet exists, it is plausible that memorable cues will also produce more efficient associative recall. In the driftdiffusion model for preferential choice (Milosavljevic et al., 2010), faster processing implies that value-related evidence for a choice option is accumulated at a faster rate. In Appendix: An alternate model, we show that the same predicted interactions between memorability and value can be derived under the assumptions that (1) more memorable cues increase the rate of evidence accumulation for associated values, and (2) values are coded relative to a reference point at the average value within an experimentfoot_6 While these assumptions are not as well mechanistically motivated as our distinctiveness-based account, they permit a theoretical link to the large literature on evidence accumulation models of preferential choice (Milosavljevic et al., 2010; Aschenbrenner et al., 1984; Diederich, 1997; Busemeyer, 1985; Busemeyer and Townsend, 1993). Indeed, memorability in our experiments functions similarly to value certainty (see, in particular, model 5 Lee and Usher, 2023) and attention (Krajbich et al., 2010; Smith and Krajbich, 2019) in other accounts of evidence accumulation in preferential choice. Cue memorability may even in part underlie the observed patterns of eye movements and subjective feelings of confidence in those settings (Sulewski et al., 2026; Broers and Busch, 2021). We also note that our distinctiveness-based account may be in principle compatible with this evidence-accumulation-based account, under additional assumptions about how exemplar retrieval unfolds over time to provide decision evidence. We hope that future work will further explore all these connections.

We then generalize our account to a more naturalistic domain: preferential food choices. In food choices, the role of explicit episodic memory retrieval is less obvious, as cues are highly familiar and values may be easily accessible after a lifetime of experiences. Still, in situations of decision deliberation, recent work has suggested that food choices recruit the same underlying associative and relational memory mechanisms, putatively to retrieve and construct value preferences (Shadlen and Shohamy, 2016; Bakkour et al., 2019). However, a recent study reported no overall bias towards preferring foods cued with more memorable images (Li et al., 2022), a finding that we replicate here.

Consistent with our theoretical account and the results of the simpler reward learning tasks, we find that cue memorability and value interact to predict food choices. In particular, when differences in value are small, choices are preferentially sensitive to the associated value of a more memorable cue (mirroring the results of Experiment 2B). This result, replicated in both published and newly-collected data, suggests that even with familiar choice options like common foods, episodic and associative memory processes (as described by our exemplar model) are recruited to construct preferences during deliberation (Bakkour et al., 2019). We also find that choices are more sensitive to value preferences when food image cues are more memorable, although this effect is considerably smaller and less reliable than the previous. One possibility is that when choice options differ more strongly in value, other (non-episodic) decision signals are more available. Multiple learning and decision systems contribute to value-based choices (Doll et al., 2015), and decision signals from the striatum or elsewhere may be more reliably recruited on easier decisions. On this account, episodic memory may be most valuable under conditions of extensive deliberation, when other sources of evidence do not discriminate between the choice options (as in the "similar-rating" trials). A related study indeed observed greater hippocampal activation during the (food) decisions with the longest response times-presumably those requiring the most deliberation (Bakkour et al., 2019). Still, across all these settings, our results provide strong evidence that shared episodic memory computations underlie both episodic associative retrieval and food choices.

We note that inferring values from cues may recruit alternate behavioral strategies in different settings. For instance, unlike in the random cue-value pairings used in our tasks, sometimes the perceptual content of a cue itself directly predicts reward outcomes (Gershman and Niv, 2010; Niv et al., 2015). In these cases, cue-target similarity can promote adaptive decision-making via generalization (Guttman and Kalish, 1956). In other cases, cues may contain rich semantic knowledge about the kind of predicted rewards, enabling rapid associations to be formed (Seligman, 1970). We predict, as in food choices, that these choices will demonstrate the same effects insofar as relational and associative memory mechanisms are recruited to construct preferences.

Our work has several shortcomings to address in future research. First, rewards in our tasks were either small (making correct choices in Experiments 1 and 2 resulted in slightly higher bonuses) or entirely noninstrumental (in Experiment 3). Second, it is possible that the effects of memorability observed here depend on the content of stimuli (Cook and Westerman, 2026); our experiments here are limited to image sets of objects (THINGSmem Kramer et al., 2023) and standard foods (Li et al., 2022). It is also unclear whether images that are associable with value are more associable with any arbitrary target stimuli. Future work should also investigate how these effects of memorability relate to the dynamics of decision-making and participant response times, drawing upon evidence accumulation models. In addition, future work may investigate how to disentangle memorability from other appetitive features of choice options; recent work using generative AI to make stimuli more or less memorable may be a promising approach (Roberts and Bainbridge, 2026).

The present work demonstrates that memorable cues are more associable with value, and lead to more value-sensitive decisions. We present a formal theory of how cue distinctiveness from other exemplars drives both memorability in recognition experiments and more reliable estimates of associative value in a similarityweighted retrieval process. This account allows us to both make and confirm key predictions about how memorability and value interact to guide decisions.

4 Methods

All five experiments, including hypotheses, sample sizes, exclusion criteria, and statistical models, were preregistered on the Open Science Framework (https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/Z9Y5F).

4.1 Participants

We recruited five independent samples of online participants via Prolific. Eligibility was restricted to Prolific users residing in the United States who reported English as their first language and had an approval rating of at least 99% on previous Prolific studies across at least 50 previous submissions. Experiments were programmed in jsPsych (version 8.2.3; (Leeuw et al., 2023)) and completed in a web browser. All participants provided informed consent, and all procedures were approved by the Columbia University Institutional Review Board. Participants received a base payment at a rate of $12/hour, with a performance-dependent bonus. We applied preregistered exclusion criteria to each sample (see Inclusion Criteria), with a final sample size of N = 100 participants per experiment.

4.2 Stimuli

Stimuli for Experiments 1 and 2 were naturalistic object photographs from the THINGS image database (Hebart et al., 2019). We used the THINGS memorability scores of Kramer et al. (2023), which were measured in an independent, large-scale continuous recognition task. Each image's score is its corrected recognition rate: the proportion of observers who correctly recognized the image on its repeat presentation (hit rate) minus the proportion who falsely reported having seen it on its first presentation (false-alarm rate).

We selected a fixed set of 468 images: for each of the 26 higher-level categories (excluding "insect" which did not have enough images), we selected 6 object concepts in each of 3 memorability bins (low, mid, high) for a total of 156 images per bin. Selection proceeded in two steps. First, for each object concept we identified one candidate exemplar image for each bin: its lowest-scoring exemplar (for the low bin), its highest-scoring exemplar (for the high bin), and its exemplar closest to the median memorability score of all THINGS images (0.795; for the mid bin). Second, we used a mixed-integer linear program (MILP) (Slivkoff and Gallant, 2021) to assign object concepts to category-bin slots, subject to the hard constraints that each category contribute exactly six concepts to each bin and that each concept be used at most once. This procedure ensured that each chosen object concept (e.g., aardvark) appeared only once in the set, and that each higher-level category (e.g., animal) was equally represented across memorability bins, while enforcing maximal distance between the three memorability bins.

In the resulting set, average memorability by bin was: low = 0.550 (0.410-0.692), mid = 0.795 (0.789-0.805), high = 0.952 (0.881-1.000). Experiment 1 used all three bins (the full 468-image set); Experiment 2 used only the low-and high-memorability bins (312 images).

In Experiment 3, following Li et al. (2022), we used images from the Food Folio stimulus set from the Columbia Center for Eating Disorders (Lloyd et al., 2020). Stimuli for the original study included all 138 images in the Food Folio dataset (Lloyd et al., 2020). To best evaluate our memorability-based hypotheses, we ranked these images using the memorability scores collected in Experiment 1A of Li et al. (2022), and only use the lowest 46 and highest 46 items by memorability score (low mean = 0.332, range -0.025-0.415; high mean = 0.607, range 0.527-0.860).

4.3 Task Design

Our experiments adapted the memory-guided decision-making task of Duncan and Shohamy (2016) (Fig. 1). On each trial, participants used a computer keyboard to choose between two cards, each depicting a unique image. Cards were 440x440-pixel squares, rendered fullscreen on a 1060x620-pixel canvas. Each choice lasted for up to 2 seconds. The chosen card was highlighted for 1 second and then flipped over to reveal its monetary value (either $0 or $1) for 1.5 seconds, followed by a 0.5-second inter-trial interval. If no response was made within 2 seconds, a "too slow" message was displayed for 1.5 seconds and a randomly selected card flipped over.

On "new" (encoding) trials, both cards depicted images the participant had not yet seen, and the participant learned the value of the card they chose. On "old" (retrieval) trials, one card (Experiments 1A and 1B) or both cards (Experiments 2A and 2B) had been chosen on an earlier new trial, and participants could use their memory of the value learned for that card to guide their response. A given image only appeared once or twice; once a card appeared on an old trial, it never appeared again. Delays between choosing a card and subsequently seeing it again on an old trial ranged from 7 to 15 trials, and were controlled to be roughly uniform (see Trial Sequencing). Participants saw 3 interspersed attention check trials, which included a hidden prompt to identify AI agents. Both attention check failures and AI catch responses resulted in data exclusion.

Trial Sequencing For Experiments 1A, 1B, 2A, and 2B, the trial sequence was fixed in advance in order to precisely balance experimental variables and potential confounds (old/new trial type, memorability bin, delay, and value). Trial sequences were created with a two-phase MILP process. Phase 1 determined which trials were old or new, with no more than 8 consecutive trials of the same type, and, for each old trial, which earlier new trial supplied its old card (or cards). Phase 2 assigned each trial's memorability bin and value, with no more than 3 consecutive trials from the same memorability bin. Phase 2 also matched the distributions of encoding-retrieval delays and left/right positions exactly across memorability bins and values, so that these potential confounds were orthogonal to our main analyses.

We pre-computed ten independent solved sequences per experiment (Experiment 1B reused Experiment 1A's ten sequences), and each participant was randomly assigned one of these ten sequences. While memorability bins were fixed by these sequences, the particular images assigned to each trial were randomized for each participant.

Experiment 1A

In Experiment 1A, we tested whether image memorability produces stronger memory for associated values. The task consisted of 312 trials, involving all 468 images. Half of the trials were new, and half were old. The two images on any given trial were always drawn from the same memorability bin. On each old trial, the participant saw one previously-chosen card and one novel card (52 old trials per memorability bin, half with a $1 old card and half with $0). Choices on old trials were labeled "accurate" if a participant chose an old card worth $1 or avoided an old card worth $0 (i.e., chose the novel card).

Experiment 1B

In the old trials of Experiment 1A, a single choice reflects both recognition of the previously seen image and retrieval of its associated value; Experiment 1B separated these two components. It thereby tested whether THINGS memorability scores predict recognition in our task at all, since the scores come from an independent task (Kramer et al., 2023) with a different response format and much longer delays. Experiment 1B reused Experiment 1A's trial sequences and stimulus set exactly; only the old trials changed. On old trials, participants first had 2 seconds to make a 2-alternative-forced-choice recognition judgment ("which of these two cards have you seen before?"), and received feedback for 0.75 seconds: the chosen card was highlighted green if correct or red if incorrect. Then, regardless of whether the recognition judgment was correct, the old card was shown alone and participants had 2 seconds to make a 2-alternativeforced-choice value memory report, between $0 and $1. A 0.75-second feedback screen then indicated the correct value. This direct report measures associative memory for value separately from the recognition judgment. New trials were identical to Experiment 1A.

Experiment 2A Experiment 2A tested whether the effect of memorability on value-guided choice holds when participants choose between two previously seen cards, removing any influence of the novel foil. Each old trial showed two previously chosen cards from the same memorability bin, one worth $1 and one worth $0. Because both cards were equally familiar and from the same bin, a bias toward more memorable images could not by itself produce a correct choice. We used only the high-and low-memorability bins, giving 234 trials: 156 new trials (78 high, 78 low) and 78 old trials (39 high, 39 low). Every card chosen on a new trial reappeared on exactly one old trial. The sequence design matched delays across memorability bins and values, and counterbalanced whether the $1 or the $0 card had the longer delay. An accurate choice meant choosing the $1 card.

Experiment 2B Experiment 2B tested how memorability influences choice when the two options' values are equal. Like Experiment 2A, it used only the high-and low-memorability bins, and each old trial showed two previously chosen cards. Unlike Experiment 2A, the two cards always came from different bins (one high, one low). The task consisted of 234 trials: 156 new and 78 old. Forty of the old trials were matched-value trials, on which both cards were worth $1 (20 trials) or both were worth $0 (20 trials). Matched-value trials have no normatively correct answer, so we analyzed choices on them for biases related to the memorability of the two images. The remaining 38 old trials were mixed-value trials, on which one card was worth $1 and the other $0 (19 trials with the high-memorability card worth $1 and 19 with it worth $0). While mixed-value trials were not the focus of our analyses, they ensured that participants stayed motivated to remember card values. The sequence design matched delays separately within the matched-and mixed-value trials, so that high-and low-memorability cards had identical delay distributions, and the high-memorability card had the longer delay on exactly half of the trials of each kind.

Experiment 3

In Experiment 3, we replicated and adapted the sequential food-choice paradigm of Li et al. (2022) (Fig. 4). Specifically, we ran a version of their "Experiment 1B", including two phases (Fig. 4). First, during a self-paced rating phase, participants rated the subjective value of each food item; they were asked to imagine they had to eat the food today and reported on a continuous slider how much they would like to eat a snack-sized portion. Second, during the choice phase, participants had 3 seconds to choose between two previously-rated food items (using a computer keyboard), imagining they would eat a snack-sized portion of the selected food item. Responses faster than 300ms or slower than 3s elicited a warning message. Each trial lasted a fixed 4 seconds, including the response period, a 0.5-second highlight around the chosen item, and a fixation for the rest of the 4-second period.

After the rating phase, the 92 foods were ranked by rating phase responses. Then, following Li et al. (2022), there were two types of choice trials. Half of trials were similar-value trials, in which foods were paired by adjacent ranks (non-overlapping) to produce 46 similar-value pairs. Half of trials were distantvalue trials, pairing foods of distant ranks. Here, we deviated from the procedure of Li et al. (2022) by first binning all images into three equal-sized memorability bins (each containing 46 images), and only using the high-and low-memorability bins. Then, we separately paired high-and low-memorability foods so that each pair shared a same memorability bin (to match Experiment 2A). Finally, following the original study's procedure, we paired foods 23 ranks away to produce 46 distant-value pairs, 23 within each memorability bin. Each food appeared in one pairing for each trial type, resulting in a total of 92 choice trials, with randomly interleaved trial types. Note that differences in memorability thus arose primarily from similar-value trials, and differences in value arose primarily from distant-value trials; below, we model all trials jointly, and separately by trial type.

4.4 Inclusion Criteria

Participants were included in our final sample according to the following criteria: (1) 100% performance on instruction comprehension, AI-screening, and attention checks; (2) full experiment completion with valid responses for at least 80% of trials; and finally (3) overall performance significantly above chance (one-sided binomial test, p < 0.05). This final criterion ensured participants correctly learned and made choices based on associated values. In Experiments 1A and 2A, it was applied to all old trial choices; in Experiment 1B, to all recognition and value reports, combined; and in Experiment 2B, to choices on mixed-value trials. For Experiment 3, this performance criterion was replaced to match the inclusion criteria from Li et al. ( 2022):(1) the probability of choosing the rightmost option was not more than 2 standard deviations from the sample mean, (2) response times were above 300 and below 3000ms on more than half of trials, and (3) a positive (p < 0.05) effect of the difference in reported value between choice options on participants' choices. Note that all performance-based criteria apply only to main effects of value, and are thus orthogonal from our primary analyses of memorability. These criteria resulted in excluding 116, 25, 92, 130, and 38 participants for Experiments 1A, 1B, 2A, 2B, and 3, respectively, to achieve a sample size of 100 included participants for each.

4.5 Statistical Analysis

We modeled all outcomes with generalized linear mixed-effects models using the glmer function of the lme4 package in R (Bates et al., 2015). For models that did not converge, we followed the procedure of Bates et al. (2018) to find the maximal converging random-effects structure, using likelihood-ratio tests to select the final model. Value was coded as -1 ($0) or +1 ($1). When memorability bin had three levels (Experiments 1A and 1B), it was an ordered factor (low < mid < high) with orthogonal polynomial contrasts. When it had two levels (Experiments 2A and 2B), it was coded -1 (low) or +1 (high), which is equivalent to the linear polynomial contrast. Inference for each hypothesis was based on the Wald z-test p-value for the corresponding fixed-effect coefficient or interaction term, at α = .05, two-tailed.

Experiment 1A We modeled whether the old card was chosen as a function of its value, its memorability bin, and their interaction: where old image and new image identify the old card's and the novel foil's images. A significant old value × memorability bin interaction indicates that choices are more sensitive to the old card's value when its image is more memorable (consistent with associability). A significant main effect of memorability bin indicates that participants are more likely to choose more memorable old cards regardless of their value (consistent with a memorability bias).

Experiment 1B We modeled recognition accuracy and value-report accuracy on old trials, each as a function of the old card's value and memorability bin: glmer(correct_recognition ~old_value * memorability_bin + (old_value * memorability_bin | participant_id) + (old_value | old_image) + (1 | new_image), family = binomial) glmer(correct_recall ~old_value * memorability_bin + (old_value * memorability_bin | participant_id) + (old_value | old_image) + (1 | new_image), family = binomial)

An effect of memorability bin on recognition accuracy tests whether memorability scores predict behavior in this task, and an effect on value-report accuracy tests whether memorability predicts associability with value.

Experiment 2A We modeled choice accuracy (choosing the $1 card) as a function of the memorability bin shared by the two cards: where value1 image and value0 image identify the images of the $1 and $0 cards.

Because every image belongs to exactly one memorability bin, we did not include item-level random slopes for memorability. A significant positive effect of memorability bin indicates more accurate choices when both cards are highly memorable than when both are low in memorability.

Experiment 2B For matched-value trials, we modeled whether the high-memorability card was chosen as a function of the cards' shared value (overall value: +1 if both cards were worth $1, -1 if both were worth $0): glmer(chose_high_mem ~overall_value + (overall_value | participant_id) + (overall_value | high_mem_image) + (overall_value | low_mem_image), family = binomial) A significant positive overall value slope indicates that participants chose the more memorable card when both cards were worth $1 and avoided it when both were worth $0. A significant intercept indicates an overall bias toward the more memorable card regardless of value. A significant delta value × overall memorability interaction indicates that choices are more consistent with values when both options are more memorable. A significant delta memorability × overall value interaction indicates that participants favor the more memorable option when both options are high in value and the less memorable option when both are low. A main effect of delta memorability indicates an overall bias toward the more memorable option.

We also fit restricted versions of this model to subsets of the data. For the similar-rating trials, we fit the following model, assuming that the differences in value were approximately zero: glmer(chose_right ~ delta_memorability * overall_value + (delta_memorability * overall_value | participant_id) + (1 | right_image) + (1 | left_image), family = binomial)

For the distant-rating trials, we fit the following model, assuming that the differences in memorability were approximately zero: glmer(chose_right ~ delta_value * overall_memorability + (delta_value * overall_memorability| participant_id) + (1 | right_image) + (1 | left_image), family = binomial)

4.6 Data & Code Availability

All behavioral data and code to perform the analysis above will be shared publicly upon publication of this manuscript.

5 Appendix: Modeling

We present an account of why image memorability may predict value associability, drawing on mechanistic exemplar models of memory and comparative judgment (Hintzman, 1984; Nosofsky, 1988; Juslin and Persson, 2002; Gomilsek et al., 2025). Our account rests on three assumptions: (1) the value of a cue is recalled by retrieving similar exemplars from memory, (2) more memorable cues are more distinctive, and thus face less interference from other exemplars, and (3) choices are based on the recalled values. Our approach follows that of Gomilsek et al. (2025), who, in their "recollection-based model", use similar principles to show how cue-value repetition affects value-based decisionsfoot_7 . Our experiments, in contrast, manipulate only the properties of the cue, and thus we extend their model to explain how cue memorability can influence the downstream recall and choice processes. Together, this model yields natural predictions about interactions between memorability and value that we describe below and confirm directly in Experiments 2 and 3.

Value recall. We assume that, when a participant is in the experiment, they store the J stimuli they have seen previously in their memory, each associated with a value v j (in our Experiments 1 and 2, v j ∈ {0, 1}). On an old trial, cue i appears, and the participant then estimates its value v i by retrieving exemplars in proportion to their similarity, s ij , computed on the cues' non-value features. Value recall therefore proceeds through a cue-matching process, and the value estimate is the similarity-weighted average (

In Gomilsek et al. (2025), cues were presented repeatedly, with each repetition adding another exemplar with maximal similarity to the cue. Value retrieval then naturally becomes more accurate as this matching process becomes dominated by signal from repeated trials of the same cue-value pair. In our experiments, however, each cue is seen only once, so repetition cannot drive differences in recall accuracy. Instead, what matters is how much the cue's single trace competes with traces of other cues.

Memorability as distinctiveness.

Recent work has argued that more memorable stimuli are more distinctive (in the representational space in which memory storage and retrieval occurs) (Simpson et al., 2026; Morales-Calva et al., 2025). In providing quantitative accounts of item-level hit and false-alarm rates during recognition memory, Meagher and Nosofsky (2023); Nosofsky et al. (2025) found that it was necessary to assume that stimuli could be distinctive in a way not captured by their specific measured representational space (using multidimensional scaling), a modeling approach they call a hybrid-similarity model (Nosofsky and Zaki, 2003). For our purposes, however, this simply means that particularly memorable stimuli will be even less similar to other stored exemplars (choice options). Indeed, Simpson et al. (2026) showed that memorability is strongly correlated with a measure of distinctiveness in the THINGSmem dataset (Kramer et al., 2023), from which our stimuli are drawn. Thus, more memorable stimuli will have lower similarity to other choice options presented in the experiment, leading to the weighted sum being dominated by the true associated value for probe i rather than interference from other stimuli. This is similar to the recall benefits of distinctive cues seen in related models such as the search of associative memory (SAM) model (Gillund and Shiffrin, 1984; Raaijmakers and Shiffrin, 1981) and the model of Nairne (2002). As in previous exemplar/instance models (Brown et al., 2007; Neath and Brown, 2006), we represent the distinctiveness of cue i with a parameter

This distinctiveness is the weight placed on exemplar i's own value v i when its value is estimated from the same probe i; the remaining 1 -D i is spread across the values of other, similar exemplars, so less distinctive items are more influenced by competing memory traces. We note that this distinctiveness D i corresponds (inversely) to Shepard's probability P i of confusing stimulus i with another stimulus (Shepard, 1957), or equivalently to the probability of accurately reconstructing i given itself as a probe. Because values were randomly assigned to cues, the values of these other cues are uncorrelated with their similarity to i, so in expectation their weighted average equals the mean value in the experiment, V µ (= .5 in Experiments 1 and 2). The value estimate therefore reduces to

Recalled values are thus shrunk toward the mean, and less distinctive cues are shrunk more.

Choice. Following Gomilsek et al. (2025), we now assume that when presented with a choice between two stimuli, participants use their value estimates along with Luce (1959)'s choice rule, such that the probability of choosing option o over option p is a logistic function of the difference in recalled values:

This motivates using logistic regression to fit our choice data as a function of values, as is standard in the decision-making literature. However, because we observe only the true values (not the recalled values), we decompose this further using the recall model from above:

We define δ D = D o -D p as the difference in distinctiveness and Σ D = D o +D p as the summed distinctiveness; δ v and Σ v are the corresponding terms for value. Since D o = (Σ D + δ D )/2 and D p = (Σ D -δ D )/2, the first part of the equation becomes

Including the last term (-δ D V µ ), we get

Term (a) says that sensitivity to the value difference grows with the pair's overall distinctiveness: when both cues are distinctive, their recalled values are close to their true values, and the better option is chosen more reliably. Term (b) says that a difference in distinctiveness biases choice in a direction that depends on the pair's overall value. Because the more distinctive cue is shrunk less toward the mean, it is recalled as better than its counterpart when both cues are above the mean (Σ v > 2V µ ) and as worse when both are below it (Σ v < 2V µ ). When Σ v = 2V µ , overall distinctiveness has no effect. Finally, substituting this decomposition into the choice rule, we get

This is a logistic regression in which the effect of value difference depends on overall memorability (distinctiveness), and the effect of memorability difference depends on overall value. It yields two key predictions: 1.

Overall memorability × value difference: participants are more sensitive to value differences between memorable cues.

2. Memorability difference × overall value: participants prefer the more memorable cue when both cues are high in value and the less memorable cue when both are low in value.

The first prediction is directly tested in Experiment 2A, by varying the summed memorability of two choice options with distinct values. The second prediction is directly tested in Experiment 2B, by evaluating choices between equal-valued items with a large difference in memorability. With binary values (V µ = .5), Σ v -2V µ takes the values +1, 0, and -1, so the model predicts a "reflection effect": memorable cues are preferred in high-high pairs, avoided in low-low pairs, and memorability difference has no effect in mixed pairs. We also test for both interactions in Experiment 3. In Experiment 1, where old trials only involve one old option, we assume that participants infer the novel card's value to be the mean over the experiment ($0.50).

Notably, the model predicts no main effect of memorability difference, because memorability does not make a cue more attractive in itself; it only sharpens retrieval, making the cue's recalled value less biased by competitors. For the same reason, it predicts no main effect of overall memorability.

Lastly, we note that the relationship between memorability and distinctiveness is still an active area of research (Simpson et al., 2026; Roberts and Bainbridge, 2026; Nosofsky et al., 2025; Bainbridge, 2026; Bylinskii et al., 2015). We refer to distinctiveness here as it functions in the representational space in which memories are stored and retrieved (which is unknown), but this concept deserves more consideration in future experimental work.

An alternate model of memorability as processing efficiency. The distinctiveness-based account above provides a parsimonious explanation for the effects of memorability on choices in our data. How, though, does this model relate to traditional evidence-accumulation models of decision-making? Here, we show that under certain assumptions, the same empirical predictions about how choice relates to memorability follow from the idea that memorability enhances processing efficiency in an evidence accumulation process. Indeed, recent studies suggest that memorable stimuli are processed more quickly ( (Broers et al., 2018; Deng et al., 2024; Bainbridge et al., 2026) but see Sheffi and Makovski, 2026). In the drift-diffusion model for preferential choice (Milosavljevic et al., 2010), faster processing implies that value-related evidence for a choice option is accumulated at a faster rate. We show below that this account, that memorability speeds the accumulation of value evidence, predicts the same interactions on binary choices as the distinctiveness account above, and we outline how these alternative accounts may be compatible with each other. We note that this account is formally very similar to the one used to account for the memory bias (Gluth et al., 2015).

We assume that evidence for choice option i is retrieved with rate R i , and accumulates an average value estimate v i . The overall drift rate of the accumulator for a binary choice (between two options o and p) will be R o v o -R p v p . This has the same form as the left hand side of equation 6, and thus can be rearranged in the same way, that is: where Σ R and δ R correspond to overall rate and the difference in rate or, equivalently, overall memorability and the difference in memorability.

Following the derivation of Palmer et al. (2005), given this drift rate, the probability of choosing option o will be where σ 2 is the variance of the diffusion process.

Thus, this model predicts the same two interactions of memorability and value and choice as the distinctiveness account: between the overall memorability and difference in value, and between the overall value and difference in memorability. However, to predict the exact effects observed in Experiments 2A and 2B, this account needs one additional assumption: that in our experiment, values are encoded by a participant in a reference-dependent way (Kahneman and Tversky, 1979)-that is, that $0 is experienced as a negative reward (relative to the average reward experienced in the experiments), rather than a lack of reward. This centering is necessary to predict the reflection effect (Kahneman and Tversky, 1979; Weilbächer et al., 2020) observed in Experiments 2B, where, when both items are low-value, choices are biased towards picking the low-memorability item (see Figures 3B & 4C). In contrast, this reference-dependence naturally falls out of the distinctiveness mechanism, in which interference from memory competition pulls value estimates towards the average value of stored exemplars (in our experiments, $0.50).

For this reason, we believe the distinctiveness account presents the most parsimonious account of our data, although under certain assumptions, the two accounts could be compatible. Namely, the exemplar model lacks any notion of time, as a global match or content echo (Hintzman, 1984) computation is typically assumed to occur in fixed time. Thus, any connection between these two accounts requires an explanation of how a recalled value estimate in an exemplar model becomes evidence over time in a drift diffusion process; we leave this open question, and a thorough treatment of response times in our data, to future work.

Nevertheless, formulating the effect of memorability in an evidence accumulation framework highlights certain parallels to related research. Memorability in our experiments functions similarly to certainty (see, in particular, model 5 Lee and Usher, 2023) and attention (Krajbich et al., 2010) in other decisions described p 1B recall by whether you recognized the image

We also do not find evidence of a memorability bias in our independent sample. 6 Supplementary Figures

Figure 1 :

Figure 1: Task Design. (A) On each trial, participants chose between two cards and learned the value of the chosen card. On old trials (interleaved throughout the sequence), participants encountered previously-chosen ("old") cards and used their memory for card value to guide their choices. Images were grouped in low, middle, and high memorability bins based on data from Kramer et al. (2023). (B) Old trial structure for each experiment.In Experiment 1A, participants chose between one old card and one new card. In Experiment 1B, to disentangle recognition memory and value recall, participants first selected which card they saw before, and then which value that card was worth. In Experiment 2A, participants saw two old cards, of the same memorability bin but different value. In Experiment 2B, on "matched-value" trials, participants saw two old cards of the same value but different memorability bins, to test how memorability would bias decisions between equal-value options.

Figure 2 :

Figure 2: Cue memorability drives stronger associative recall and more value-sensitive choices. (A) In Experiment 1A, old trial choices were more sensitive to remembered value when the cue image was more memorable. (B) In Experiment 1B, old trials involved first a 2-AFC recognition memory decision and then a 2-AFC value recall. Both image recognition and value recall were stronger when the cue image was more memorable. All error bars depict 95% confidence interval of the mean.

Figure 4: Cue memorability interacts with the reported value of options to predict food choices. (A) Task design, replicating Li et al. (2022) Participants first rated the subjective value of various food images and subsequently chose between two rated food images. We reused memorability scores collected by the original study. (B) On distant-value trials, we do not find reliable evidence that choices were more consistent for cues of different memorability. (C) On similar-value trials, participants' choices reflected more sensitivity to the value of the higher-memorability item; they were more likely to choose the more memorable cues when both options were high-value, and more likely to choose the less memorable cue when both were low-value (mirroring Exp. 2B). All error bars depict 95% confidence interval of the mean.

glmer(chose_old ~old_value * memorability_bin + (old_value * memorability_bin | participant_id) + (old_value | old_image) + (1 | new_image), family = binomial)

Experiment 3

We z-scored value ratings within each participant and modeled whether the right-hand option was chosen as a function of the difference (right -left) and sum (right + left) of the two options' values and memorability scores: glmer(chose_right ~delta_value * overall_memorability + delta_memorability * overall_value + (delta_value * overall_memorability + delta_memorability * overall_value | participant_id) + (1 | right_image) + (1 | left_image), family = binomial)

FigureS2: Experiment 1B -Value Recall by Recognition Performance. Participants value recall was stronger on trials where they correctly recognized the old card, and not on trials where they failed to recognize the old card (only in the replication data).

Independent pilot samples support all main results. (A) In Experiment 1A, participants choices were more sensitive to value for more memorable cue images. (C) In Experiment 2A, participants choices were more sensitive to value for more memorable cue images. (D) In Experiment 2B, on matched-value trials,

While a notion of memorability as a population-level property of an item can be generalized across stimulus types and memory tests, we follow much of the extant literature (starting withIsola et al., 2011), in operationalizing memorability as the corrected hit rate for a particular image in a continuous recognition task 2 Indeed, the idea that certain cues might promote conditioning of certain stimuli has a long history in studies of animal learning(Seligman, 1970).

As there are only three memorability bins, a quadratic term is the only possible nonlinear orthogonal contrast so we refer to it as nonlinear throughout.

We note that there is a growing literature on the relationship between target distinctiveness (or the distribution of targets) and judgments and decision making(Choplin and Hummel, 2002; Brown and Matthews, 2011; Choplin and Wedell, 2014)

In the Appendix, we also describe how this model may relate to an alternative account of memorability as modulating evidence accumulation in a drift diffusion process, and how these two accounts may be related.

This may be related to screen-position bias sometimes observed in memory and decision-making tasks(Jou et al., 2016; Krajbich et al., 2010), where (English-speaking) participants typically look at the leftmost option first (likely because they read left to right), and often respond based only on the leftmost stimulus(Simon, 1955, a kind of "satisficing")

This additional assumption is naturally handled in distinctiveness-based account, where values are implicitly meanreferenced by the similarity-weighted retrieval process.

This model also has overlapping ideas withBordalo et al. (2020)'s similarity-based retrieval model in economics
